Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well... I guess the main thing is that I just stopped caring about everything I used to look forward to. Like, my fishing gear is still sitting untouched out in the garage. I was supposed to take it out to the lake a couple weekends ago, but it just sat there. It's right next to my workbench, actually. I had this whole system set up for organizing my tools, which reminds me of the supply chain mess we've been dealing with at work. I'm managing this new commercial build off Route 9, and the subcontractors are constantly pointing fingers at each other over delayed shipments. I spent three hours on the phone Tuesday just arguing with a guy about steel beams. Just endless back-and-forth and no one wants to take responsibility for the schedule... 

But, uh, anyway, where was I? Right, the garage. Yeah, the woodshop out there just feels like a massive chore now. I don't even want to look at it. And it's not just that... even when my kids try to get me to play chess in the evenings, I’m just going through the motions. I just don't have it in me anymore.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I'd say... it's been about a year and seven months. Basically since my wife’s health problems started and just changed everything at home. 

We had to get all this new medical equipment for the house, and dealing with the insurance company for it has been an absolute nightmare. It’s honestly worse than dealing with the city permit office. At least with the city, you know which clerk to butter up with a box of donuts, you know? But these insurance reps just read from a script. It reminds me of this safety inspector we had on site last month. Guy walks around with his little clipboard, completely ignoring the actual structural hazards, just looking to check his little boxes. He tried to cite us for an extension cord that wasn't even plugged in. I had to walk him over to the generator and physically show him that the cord was dead, and he still wanted to argue with me about the OSHA manual... 

But, uh... sorry. My brain is just... I don't know. What were we talking about? Oh, right, how long. Yeah, about nineteen months since she got sick. That's when I stopped caring.

No, not really. That's the thing. I'm just... I'm just empty when I do them. Like the chess with the kids. I move the pieces, but I don't care if I win or lose. I used to love teaching them strategy, you know? Setting up traps, showing them how to control the center of the board. It’s a lot like managing a job site, actually. If you don't stage your materials right, your framing crew is just standing around costing you money while they wait for the lumber delivery. And don't even get me started on the concrete guys. They always want to pour on a Friday afternoon, which means if the inspector is running late, you're paying overtime for everyone to just sit there and watch it cure. I had a guy last year try to tell me the mix was too dry, and I had to get the slump cone out myself to prove him wrong...

Wait, what did you ask me? Oh, about enjoying things. Right. No, there's no pleasure in it. The chess, the fishing, the woodshop... it all just feels like work now. I'm just doing it because I feel like I'm supposed to, not because I want to.

What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well... I guess the main thing is that I just stopped caring about everything I used to look forward to. Like, my fishing gear is still sitting untouched out in the garage. I was supposed to take it out to the lake a couple weekends ago, but it just sat there. It's right next to my workbench, actually. I had this whole system set up for organizing my tools, which reminds me of the supply chain mess we've been dealing with at work. I'm managing this new commercial build off Route 9, and the subcontractors are constantly pointing fingers at each other over delayed shipments. I spent three hours on the phone Tuesday just arguing with a guy about steel beams. Just endless back-and-forth and no one wants to take responsibility for the schedule... \n\nBut, uh, anyway, where was I? Right, the garage. Yeah, the woodshop out there just feels like a massive chore now. I don't even want to look at it. And it's not just that... even when my kids try to get me to play chess in the evenings, I’m just going through the motions. I just don't have it in me anymore.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this way, like you've stopped caring about things you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: I'd say... it's been about a year and seven months. Basically since my wife’s health problems started and just changed everything at home. \n\nWe had to get all this new medical equipment for the house, and dealing with the insurance company for it has been an absolute nightmare. It’s honestly worse than dealing with the city permit office. At least with the city, you know which clerk to butter up with a box of donuts, you know? But these insurance reps just read from a script. It reminds me of this safety inspector we had on site last month. Guy walks around with his little clipboard, completely ignoring the actual structural hazards, just looking to check his little boxes. He tried to cite us for an extension cord that wasn't even plugged in. I had to walk him over to the generator and physically show him that the cord was dead, and he still wanted to argue with me about the OSHA manual... \n\nBut, uh... sorry. My brain is just... I don't know. What were we talking about? Oh, right, how long. Yeah, about nineteen months since she got sick. That's when I stopped caring.\n\nDoctor: Do you still find any pleasure in the things you're doing, even if you're just going through the motions?\n\nPatient: No, not really. That's the thing. I'm just... I'm just empty when I do them. Like the chess with the kids. I move the pieces, but I don't care if I win or lose. I used to love teaching them strategy, you know? Setting up traps, showing them how to control the center of the board. It’s a lot like managing a job site, actually. You have to anticipate where the bottlenecks are going to be before they happen. If you don't stage your materials right, your framing crew is just standing around costing you money while they wait for the lumber delivery. And don't even get me started on the concrete guys. They always want to pour on a Friday afternoon, which means if the inspector is running late, you're paying overtime for everyone to just sit there and watch it cure. I had a guy last year try to tell me the mix was too dry, and I had to get the slump cone out myself to prove him wrong...\n\nWait, what did you ask me? Oh, about enjoying things. Right. No, there's no pleasure in it. The chess, the fishing, the woodshop... it all just feels like work now. I'm just doing it because I feel like I'm supposed to, not because I want to.\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you find yourself moving or speaking more slowly than you used to, or do you feel restless and can't sit still?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. I haven't really noticed anything like that. I mean, maybe I'm a little slower getting out of bed, but I don't think I'm moving or talking in slow motion or anything. And I'm not pacing the halls, if that's what you mean. Though, speaking of pacing, you should see the new apprentice we got on the Route 9 job. Kid cannot stand still for two seconds. He's always bouncing on his heels, twirling his hammer... it drives the older guys crazy. It's like these young guys come out of trade school thinking it's all go, go, go, but construction is a marathon, not a sprint. You have to pace yourself, especially in the summer heat. We had a guy pass out last July because he thought he could pound energy drinks instead of water... 

But, uh, anyway. What was the question? Oh, me moving slow or being restless. Yeah, no, I haven't really noticed anything like that with myself.

Basically since my wife’s health problems started and just changed everything at home. \n\nWe had to get all this new medical equipment for the house, and dealing with the insurance company for it has been an absolute nightmare. I'm just... I'm just empty when I do them. Like the chess with the kids. I move the pieces, but I don't care if I win or lose. I used to love teaching them strategy, you know? If you don't stage your materials right, your framing crew is just standing around costing you money while they wait for the lumber delivery. And don't even get me started on the concrete guys. They always want to pour on a Friday afternoon, which means if the inspector is running late, you're paying overtime for everyone to just sit there and watch it cure. I had a guy last year try to tell me the mix was too dry, and I had to get the slump cone out myself to prove him wrong...\n\nWait, what did you ask me? Oh, about enjoying things. Right. No, there's no pleasure in it. The chess, the fishing, the woodshop... it all just feels like work now. I'm just doing it because I feel like I'm supposed to, not because I want to.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself moving or speaking more slowly than you used to, or do you feel restless and can't sit still?\n\nPatient: I haven't really noticed anything like that. I mean, maybe I'm a little slower getting out of bed, but I don't think I'm moving or talking in slow motion or anything. And I'm not pacing the halls, if that's what you mean. Though, speaking of pacing, you should see the new apprentice we got on the Route 9 job. Kid cannot stand still for two seconds. He's always bouncing on his heels, twirling his hammer... it drives the older guys crazy. I keep telling him, \"Look, kid, you're burning energy you're gonna need at 3 PM when we're trying to finish framing the second floor.\" It's like these young guys come out of trade school thinking it's all go, go, go, but construction is a marathon, not a sprint. You have to pace yourself, especially in the summer heat. We had a guy pass out last July because he thought he could pound energy drinks instead of water... \n\nBut, uh, anyway. What was the question? Oh, me moving slow or being restless. Yeah, no, I haven't really noticed anything like that with myself.\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you often feel guilty or blame yourself for things that have happened?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah. Yeah, all the time. I just... I keep thinking I've completely failed my wife, you know? And the kids. And even the guys at work. If anything goes wrong—like a missed doctor's appointment for her, or a cost overrun on a job, or even a minor safety issue—I just take it as proof that I'm completely useless. Like everyone would just be better off without me in charge of things. 

Speaking of cost overruns, the price of copper pipe right now is just absolutely insane. We had to re-bid this entire plumbing phase on a commercial build last week because the supplier jacked up the rates by twenty percent overnight. Twenty percent! It completely blew up the budget. And the client, of course, thinks we're trying to fleece them. I had to sit in this three-hour meeting with their architects—who, by the way, designed a drainage system that defies the laws of gravity—trying to explain supply chain economics to them. It's like talking to a brick wall. Actually, a brick wall would be better, because at least a mason knows how to lay it straight. These architects draw lines on a computer and expect the real world to just bend to their software...

Uh... wait, what was I saying? Oh, right. Guilt. Yeah, I just... I feel like a failure. Like I'm letting everyone down, and it's all my fault.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah... yeah, I do. Especially on those early drives. I usually head out to the site around 5 AM, before anyone else is even awake. And I just catch myself thinking it would be so much easier if I just didn't make it there. 

The roads are completely empty then, you know? Just me and the heavy haulers. Actually, speaking of the haulers, the DOT completely botched the routing for that new overpass project on 95. They shut down the main artery, so now you've got these massive flatbeds carrying steel girders trying to navigate two-lane county roads. It’s ridiculous. I was stuck behind one for forty-five minutes on Tuesday. And the worst part is, we had a crane operator sitting idle at the site, and those guys charge out at two hundred bucks an hour, minimum four hours. You can't just tell a crane operator to clock out while you wait for the steel to show up. The logistics on this job are just a total nightmare right now. The general contractor didn't even bother to secure the wide-load permits until the day before, so now everyone is scrambling and pointing fingers...

Wait, sorry. I'm doing it again. What did you ask me? Oh. Right. Waking up. 

Yeah, on those early drives in the dark... sometimes the thought of just steering my truck off the road... it scares me. Because it just feels too close, you know? Like it really wouldn't take much at all.

I'm just doing it because I feel like I'm supposed to, not because I want to.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself moving or speaking more slowly than you used to, or do you feel restless and can't sit still?\n\nPatient: I haven't really noticed anything like that. I mean, maybe I'm a little slower getting out of bed, but I don't think I'm moving or talking in slow motion or anything. And I'm not pacing the halls, if that's what you mean. Though, speaking of pacing, you should see the new apprentice we got on the Route 9 job. Kid cannot stand still for two seconds. He's always bouncing on his heels, twirling his hammer... it drives the older guys crazy. I keep telling him, \"Look, kid, you're burning energy you're gonna need at 3 PM when we're trying to finish framing the second floor.\" It's like these young guys come out of trade school thinking it's all go, go, go, but construction is a marathon, not a sprint. You have to pace yourself, especially in the summer heat. We had a guy pass out last July because he thought he could pound energy drinks instead of water... \n\nBut, uh, anyway. What was the question? Oh, me moving slow or being restless. Yeah, no, I haven't really noticed anything like that with myself.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel guilty or blame yourself for things that have happened?\n\nPatient: Yeah. Yeah, all the time. I just... I keep thinking I've completely failed my wife, you know? And the kids. And even the guys at work. Like everyone would just be better off without me in charge of things. \n\nSpeaking of cost overruns, the price of copper pipe right now is just absolutely insane. And the client, of course, thinks we're trying to fleece them. I had to sit in this three-hour meeting with their architects—who, by the way, designed a drainage system that defies the laws of gravity—trying to explain supply chain economics to them. No, no... I haven't actually made a plan or anything. It's just... thoughts, you know? I haven't figured out a way or set a date or whatever. 

Though, speaking of dates and schedules, I don't even know how I'd find the time to plan anything right now. The scheduling software the front office is making us use is a complete joke. They rolled out this new cloud-based system last month, supposed to streamline everything, right? Supposed to sync the sub-contractors, the material deliveries, the inspections... all in one place. But the interface is so clunky, it takes me twice as long just to log a daily report. And it keeps crashing! Just yesterday, I spent an hour inputting the drywall schedule for the south wing, and the whole thing just froze. Poof. Gone. I had to call IT, and you know how that goes. You get some guy in a call center who asks if you've tried turning it off and on again. I'm standing there in a hard hat, trying to coordinate thirty guys who are waiting on me, and I'm supposed to reboot my tablet while the clock is ticking. It's ridiculous. The old whiteboards we used in the trailer worked perfectly fine for twenty years, but no, we have to "modernize" because some VP went to a seminar...

Uh, sorry. My mind just keeps wandering today. What were we talking about? Plans? Right. No, I haven't... I haven't made any plans to actually do it. It's just the thoughts.

I'm just doing it because I feel like I'm supposed to, not because I want to.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself moving or speaking more slowly than you used to, or do you feel restless and can't sit still?\n\nPatient: I haven't really noticed anything like that. Though, speaking of pacing, you should see the new apprentice we got on the Route 9 job. Kid cannot stand still for two seconds. He's always bouncing on his heels, twirling his hammer... it drives the older guys crazy. I keep telling him, \"Look, kid, you're burning energy you're gonna need at 3 PM when we're trying to finish framing the second floor.\" It's like these young guys come out of trade school thinking it's all go, go, go, but construction is a marathon, not a sprint. Yeah, no, I haven't really noticed anything like that with myself.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel guilty or blame yourself for things that have happened?\n\nPatient: Yeah. Yeah, all the time. I just... I keep thinking I've completely failed my wife, you know? And the kids. And even the guys at work. If anything goes wrong—like a missed doctor's appointment for her, or a cost overrun on a job, or even a minor safety issue—I just take it as proof that I'm completely useless. Like everyone would just be better off without me in charge of things. \n\nSpeaking of cost overruns, the price of copper pipe right now is just absolutely insane. We had to re-bid this entire plumbing phase on a commercial build last week because the supplier jacked up the rates by twenty percent overnight. Twenty percent! It completely blew up the budget. And the client, of course, thinks we're trying to fleece them. I had to sit in this three-hour meeting with their architects—who, by the way, designed a drainage system that defies the laws of gravity—trying to explain supply chain economics to them. It's like talking to a brick wall. Actually, a brick wall would be better, because at least a mason knows how to lay it straight. These architects draw lines on a computer and expect the real world to just bend to their software...\n\nUh... wait, what was I saying? Oh, right. Guilt. Yeah, I just... I feel like a failure. Like I'm letting everyone down, and it's all my fault.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever thought that you'd be better off dead or wished you wouldn't wake up in the morning?\n\nPatient: Yeah... yeah, I do. Especially on those early drives. I usually head out to the site around 5 AM, before anyone else is even awake. Actually, speaking of the haulers, the DOT completely botched the routing for that new overpass project on 95. They shut down the main artery, so now you've got these massive flatbeds carrying steel girders trying to navigate two-lane county roads. It’s ridiculous. And the worst part is, we had a crane operator sitting idle at the site, and those guys charge out at two hundred bucks an hour, minimum four hours. I haven't actually made a plan or anything. It's just... thoughts, you know? The scheduling software the front office is making us use is a complete joke. They rolled out this new cloud-based system last month, supposed to streamline everything, right? Supposed to sync the sub-contractors, the material deliveries, the inspections... all in one place. But the interface is so clunky, it takes me twice as long just to log a daily report. And it keeps crashing! Just yesterday, I spent an hour inputting the drywall schedule for the south wing, and the whole thing just froze. Poof. Gone. I had to call IT, and you know how that goes. You get some guy in a call center who asks if you've tried turning it off and on again. I'm standing there in a hard hat, trying to coordinate thirty guys who are waiting on me, and I'm supposed to reboot my tablet while the clock is ticking. It's ridiculous. The old whiteboards we used in the trailer worked perfectly fine for twenty years, but no, we have to \"modernize\" because some VP went to a seminar...\n\nUh, sorry. My mind just keeps wandering today. What were we talking about? Plans? Right. No, I haven't... I haven't made any plans to actually do it. It's just the thoughts.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any significant changes in your appetite or sleep patterns recently?\n\nPatient: Appetite? Yeah, it's... I don't really have one right now. Most days food just tastes like cardboard, honestly. I'll completely skip breakfast, and then even if I've been on my feet at a job site all day, I just sort of pick at my dinner. I've actually had to bring my belt in a couple of notches lately, and I'm not even trying to lose weight. \n\nThough, speaking of eating at the site... the lunch truck we usually get, this guy Sal who runs it? He's been getting absolutely hammered by city code enforcement. They completely changed the commercial parking zones on the west side of the project last month, so now he can't park by the main gate anymore. He has to set up three blocks down the street. And my guys are furious, because they only get thirty minutes for lunch, right? So it takes them ten minutes just to walk down there and back. The union steward actually barged into my trailer yesterday threatening to file a formal grievance over it. Like I have any control over municipal parking ordinances! I'm trying to coordinate a fifty-yard concrete pour, and this guy is yelling at me about meatball subs. I told him to go take it up with the mayor. It's just constant... everyone expects you to solve their problems for them, no matter how ridiculous...\n\nUh... sorry. I completely lost my train of thought again. What did you ask me? Sleep? ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Better? No... no, not really. It's just been a constant heavy fog, every single day. I can't remember a day in the last couple of weeks where I woke up and actually felt... okay. 

Although, speaking of weeks... the timeline on this current build is completely shot. We were supposed to have the framing done by the 15th, but the lumber yard shorted us on the two-by-sixes. And not just by a little bit, they missed an entire pallet. So I've got my framing crew standing around, twiddling their thumbs, while I'm on the phone with the supplier trying to track down this missing pallet. And the guy on the phone—I swear he had no idea what he was doing—he kept telling me it was marked as delivered. I'm looking at the drop zone, and I'm telling him, "Buddy, there is no wood here." Turns out, the driver dropped it at a completely different job site three miles away. Some residential development. So then we had to send one of our guys with a flatbed to go retrieve our own materials from a competitor's site. The site super over there was giving him a hard time about it, too, like we were trying to steal from them. I had to get on the phone with him and explain that his supplier is an idiot... 

Wait. Sorry. I'm rambling again. What were we talking about? Feeling better? Yeah. No, I haven't had any days where I felt better. It's just... it's all just heavy. All the time.

Yeah, I... I guess so. Mostly in the mornings. On those early drives before anyone else is awake, I'm just sitting in the truck and... 

Actually, speaking of the drive, the city has completely torn up the interstate near the downtown exit. It is a total nightmare. They've had the right two lanes closed for three weeks for this repaving project, but I swear I haven't seen a single crew out there actually working since last Tuesday. They just leave the orange barrels up and these massive concrete barriers that pinch the lane down to almost nothing. And the guys driving those big dump trucks? They don't care, they just merge right over without looking. I had to slam on my brakes the other day because some guy pulling a trailer full of bobcat attachments just cut across the solid white line. It's like nobody knows how to drive in a construction zone anymore. And the suspension on my rig is already shot from hauling heavy gear around the dirt lots, so hitting those uneven pavement transitions at sixty miles an hour feels like I'm snapping an axle every single morning. I told our fleet manager we need to get the shocks replaced, but he's dragging his feet on the purchase order, trying to save a few bucks...

Sorry. Man, I'm doing it again. My head is just... it's all over the place. What did you ask? Thoughts about... right. Self-harm. 

So, yeah, on those early drives... I just catch myself thinking it would be so much easier if I just didn't make it there. Just... didn't arrive. And sometimes the thought of just steering off the road... it scares me, honestly. Because it feels too close. Like I might actually just let the wheel go.

I haven't actually made a plan or anything. It's just... thoughts, you know? Weight? Yeah, I think I mentioned it earlier, actually. I'm definitely losing weight. I've had to pull my belt in a couple of notches lately, and I'm not even trying to. It's just... food tastes like cardboard most days, so I'll skip breakfast entirely, and then I just pick at whatever I have for dinner, even after being on my feet at the site all day.

Speaking of the site, we had the city inspector come out yesterday for the rough-in plumbing inspection on the second floor. And this guy—I swear he just likes to hear himself talk—he spent forty-five minutes lecturing my plumbing sub about the exact angle of the PVC venting. Not that it was wrong, mind you, just that he preferred a different style of routing. My plumber, Gary, he's been doing this for thirty years, and he's just standing there, biting his tongue, turning bright red. I honestly thought Gary was going to throw a pipe wrench at the guy. And the worst part is, the inspector wouldn't sign off on the permit until we moved this one vent pipe literally two inches to the left. Two inches! It doesn't change a single thing about how the system functions, but it means Gary has to rip out a whole section of drywall we just put up, cut the pipe, add two new elbows, and glue it all back together. It set us back an entire day, and now the drywallers are mad because their schedule is messed up, and I have to pay them extra to come back and patch the hole. It's just a domino effect of incompetence...

Uh, sorry. I'm doing it again, aren't I? Just... drifting off. What was the question? Weight loss. Right. Yeah, my belt is definitely looser. I just don't have the energy to eat, really.

I just don't have it in me anymore.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this way, like you've stopped caring about things you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: I'd say... it's been about a year and seven months. Basically since my wife’s health problems started and just changed everything at home. \n\nWe had to get all this new medical equipment for the house, and dealing with the insurance company for it has been an absolute nightmare. It’s honestly worse than dealing with the city permit office. At least with the city, you know which clerk to butter up with a box of donuts, you know? But these insurance reps just read from a script. It reminds me of this safety inspector we had on site last month. Guy walks around with his little clipboard, completely ignoring the actual structural hazards, just looking to check his little boxes. He tried to cite us for an extension cord that wasn't even plugged in. I had to walk him over to the generator and physically show him that the cord was dead, and he still wanted to argue with me about the OSHA manual... \n\nBut, uh... sorry. My brain is just... I don't know. What were we talking about? Oh, right, how long. Yeah, about nineteen months since she got sick. That's when I stopped caring.\n\nDoctor: Do you still find any pleasure in the things you're doing, even if you're just going through the motions?\n\nPatient: No, not really. That's the thing. I'm just... I'm just empty when I do them. Like the chess with the kids. I move the pieces, but I don't care if I win or lose. I used to love teaching them strategy, you know? Setting up traps, showing them how to control the center of the board. It’s a lot like managing a job site, actually. You have to anticipate where the bottlenecks are going to be before they happen. If you don't stage your materials right, your framing crew is just standing around costing you money while they wait for the lumber delivery. And don't even get me started on the concrete guys. They always want to pour on a Friday afternoon, which means if the inspector is running late, you're paying overtime for everyone to just sit there and watch it cure. I had a guy last year try to tell me the mix was too dry, and I had to get the slump cone out myself to prove him wrong...\n\nWait, what did you ask me? Oh, about enjoying things. Right. No, there's no pleasure in it. The chess, the fishing, the woodshop... it all just feels like work now. I'm just doing it because I feel like I'm supposed to, not because I want to.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself moving or speaking more slowly than you used to, or do you feel restless and can't sit still?\n\nPatient: I haven't really noticed anything like that. I mean, maybe I'm a little slower getting out of bed, but I don't think I'm moving or talking in slow motion or anything. And I'm not pacing the halls, if that's what you mean. Though, speaking of pacing, you should see the new apprentice we got on the Route 9 job. Kid cannot stand still for two seconds. He's always bouncing on his heels, twirling his hammer... it drives the older guys crazy. I keep telling him, \"Look, kid, you're burning energy you're gonna need at 3 PM when we're trying to finish framing the second floor.\" It's like these young guys come out of trade school thinking it's all go, go, go, but construction is a marathon, not a sprint. You have to pace yourself, especially in the summer heat. We had a guy pass out last July because he thought he could pound energy drinks instead of water... \n\nBut, uh, anyway. What was the question? Oh, me moving slow or being restless. Yeah, no, I haven't really noticed anything like that with myself.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel guilty or blame yourself for things that have happened?\n\nPatient: Yeah. Yeah, all the time. I just... I keep thinking I've completely failed my wife, you know? And the kids. And even the guys at work. If anything goes wrong—like a missed doctor's appointment for her, or a cost overrun on a job, or even a minor safety issue—I just take it as proof that I'm completely useless. Like everyone would just be better off without me in charge of things. \n\nSpeaking of cost overruns, the price of copper pipe right now is just absolutely insane. We had to re-bid this entire plumbing phase on a commercial build last week because the supplier jacked up the rates by twenty percent overnight. Twenty percent! It completely blew up the budget. And the client, of course, thinks we're trying to fleece them. I had to sit in this three-hour meeting with their architects—who, by the way, designed a drainage system that defies the laws of gravity—trying to explain supply chain economics to them. It's like talking to a brick wall. Actually, a brick wall would be better, because at least a mason knows how to lay it straight. These architects draw lines on a computer and expect the real world to just bend to their software...\n\nUh... wait, what was I saying? Oh, right. Guilt. Yeah, I just... I feel like a failure. Like I'm letting everyone down, and it's all my fault.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever thought that you'd be better off dead or wished you wouldn't wake up in the morning?\n\nPatient: Yeah... yeah, I do. Especially on those early drives. I usually head out to the site around 5 AM, before anyone else is even awake. And I just catch myself thinking it would be so much easier if I just didn't make it there. \n\nThe roads are completely empty then, you know? Just me and the heavy haulers. Actually, speaking of the haulers, the DOT completely botched the routing for that new overpass project on 95. They shut down the main artery, so now you've got these massive flatbeds carrying steel girders trying to navigate two-lane county roads. It’s ridiculous. I was stuck behind one for forty-five minutes on Tuesday. And the worst part is, we had a crane operator sitting idle at the site, and those guys charge out at two hundred bucks an hour, minimum four hours. You can't just tell a crane operator to clock out while you wait for the steel to show up. The logistics on this job are just a total nightmare right now. The general contractor didn't even bother to secure the wide-load permits until the day before, so now everyone is scrambling and pointing fingers...\n\nWait, sorry. I'm doing it again. What did you ask me? Oh. Right. Waking up. \n\nYeah, on those early drives in the dark... sometimes the thought of just steering my truck off the road... it scares me. Because it just feels too close, you know? Like it really wouldn't take much at all.\n\nDoctor: Have you made any plans for how you would end your life if you decided to go through with it?\n\nPatient: No, no... I haven't actually made a plan or anything. It's just... thoughts, you know? I haven't figured out a way or set a date or whatever. \n\nThough, speaking of dates and schedules, I don't even know how I'd find the time to plan anything right now. The scheduling software the front office is making us use is a complete joke. They rolled out this new cloud-based system last month, supposed to streamline everything, right? Supposed to sync the sub-contractors, the material deliveries, the inspections... all in one place. But the interface is so clunky, it takes me twice as long just to log a daily report. And it keeps crashing! Just yesterday, I spent an hour inputting the drywall schedule for the south wing, and the whole thing just froze. Poof. Gone. I had to call IT, and you know how that goes. You get some guy in a call center who asks if you've tried turning it off and on again. I'm standing there in a hard hat, trying to coordinate thirty guys who are waiting on me, and I'm supposed to reboot my tablet while the clock is ticking. It's ridiculous. The old whiteboards we used in the trailer worked perfectly fine for twenty years, but no, we have to \"modernize\" because some VP went to a seminar...\n\nUh, sorry. My mind just keeps wandering today. What were we talking about? Plans? Right. No, I haven't... I haven't made any plans to actually do it. It's just the thoughts.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any significant changes in your appetite or sleep patterns recently?\n\nPatient: Appetite? Yeah, it's... I don't really have one right now. Most days food just tastes like cardboard, honestly. I'll completely skip breakfast, and then even if I've been on my feet at a job site all day, I just sort of pick at my dinner. I've actually had to bring my belt in a couple of notches lately, and I'm not even trying to lose weight. \n\nThough, speaking of eating at the site... the lunch truck we usually get, this guy Sal who runs it? He's been getting absolutely hammered by city code enforcement. They completely changed the commercial parking zones on the west side of the project last month, so now he can't park by the main gate anymore. He has to set up three blocks down the street. And my guys are furious, because they only get thirty minutes for lunch, right? So it takes them ten minutes just to walk down there and back. The union steward actually barged into my trailer yesterday threatening to file a formal grievance over it. Like I have any control over municipal parking ordinances! I'm trying to coordinate a fifty-yard concrete pour, and this guy is yelling at me about meatball subs. I told him to go take it up with the mayor. It's just constant... everyone expects you to solve their problems for them, no matter how ridiculous...\n\nUh... sorry. I completely lost my train of thought again. What did you ask me? Sleep? Or... oh, appetite. Right. Yeah, I just... I don't eat much. Like I said, nothing really tastes good anyway.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any periods in the past two weeks where you felt somewhat better, even if only for a few days?\n\nPatient: Better? No... no, not really. It's just been a constant heavy fog, every single day. I can't remember a day in the last couple of weeks where I woke up and actually felt... okay. \n\nAlthough, speaking of weeks... the timeline on this current build is completely shot. We were supposed to have the framing done by the 15th, but the lumber yard shorted us on the two-by-sixes. And not just by a little bit, they missed an entire pallet. So I've got my framing crew standing around, twiddling their thumbs, while I'm on the phone with the supplier trying to track down this missing pallet. And the guy on the phone—I swear he had no idea what he was doing—he kept telling me it was marked as delivered. I'm looking at the drop zone, and I'm telling him, \"Buddy, there is no wood here.\" Turns out, the driver dropped it at a completely different job site three miles away. Some residential development. So then we had to send one of our guys with a flatbed to go retrieve our own materials from a competitor's site. The site super over there was giving him a hard time about it, too, like we were trying to steal from them. I had to get on the phone with him and explain that his supplier is an idiot... \n\nWait. Sorry. I'm rambling again. What were we talking about? Feeling better? Yeah. No, I haven't had any days where I felt better. It's just... it's all just heavy. All the time.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any thoughts about self-harm or suicide in the past week?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I... I guess so. Mostly in the mornings. On those early drives before anyone else is awake, I'm just sitting in the truck and... \n\nActually, speaking of the drive, the city has completely torn up the interstate near the downtown exit. It is a total nightmare. They've had the right two lanes closed for three weeks for this repaving project, but I swear I haven't seen a single crew out there actually working since last Tuesday. They just leave the orange barrels up and these massive concrete barriers that pinch the lane down to almost nothing. And the guys driving those big dump trucks? They don't care, they just merge right over without looking. I had to slam on my brakes the other day because some guy pulling a trailer full of bobcat attachments just cut across the solid white line. It's like nobody knows how to drive in a construction zone anymore. And the suspension on my rig is already shot from hauling heavy gear around the dirt lots, so hitting those uneven pavement transitions at sixty miles an hour feels like I'm snapping an axle every single morning. I told our fleet manager we need to get the shocks replaced, but he's dragging his feet on the purchase order, trying to save a few bucks...\n\nSorry. Man, I'm doing it again. My head is just... it's all over the place. What did you ask? Thoughts about... right. Self-harm. \n\nSo, yeah, on those early drives... I just catch myself thinking it would be so much easier if I just didn't make it there. Just... didn't arrive. And sometimes the thought of just steering off the road... it scares me, honestly. Because it feels too close. Like I might actually just let the wheel go.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any significant weight loss or gain recently?\n\nPatient: Weight? Yeah, I think I mentioned it earlier, actually. I'm definitely losing weight. I've had to pull my belt in a couple of notches lately, and I'm not even trying to. It's just... food tastes like cardboard most days, so I'll skip breakfast entirely, and then I just pick at whatever I have for dinner, even after being on my feet at the site all day.\n\nSpeaking of the site, we had the city inspector come out yesterday for the rough-in plumbing inspection on the second floor. And this guy—I swear he just likes to hear himself talk—he spent forty-five minutes lecturing my plumbing sub about the exact angle of the PVC venting. Not that it was wrong, mind you, just that he preferred a different style of routing. My plumber, Gary, he's been doing this for thirty years, and he's just standing there, biting his tongue, turning bright red. I honestly thought Gary was going to throw a pipe wrench at the guy. And the worst part is, the inspector wouldn't sign off on the permit until we moved this one vent pipe literally two inches to the left. Two inches! It doesn't change a single thing about how the system functions, but it means Gary has to rip out a whole section of drywall we just put up, cut the pipe, add two new elbows, and glue it all back together. It set us back an entire day, and now the drywallers are mad because their schedule is messed up, and I have to pay them extra to come back and patch the hole. It's just a domino effect of incompetence...\n\nUh, sorry. I'm doing it again, aren't I? Just... drifting off. What was the question? Weight loss. Right. Yeah, my belt is definitely looser. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Moving slow? Um... I don't know, maybe? I guess I haven't really noticed if I'm moving slower, or if I'm restless. I just feel exhausted. 

Although, speaking of moving slow, you should see the concrete crew we hired for the foundation pour on the new commercial lot. I mean, these guys are moving like molasses in January. We had the pump truck scheduled for 7:00 AM sharp on Tuesday, right? Because you have to beat the heat, otherwise the mix starts setting up too fast in the hose. Well, 7:00 AM rolls around, no pump truck. 7:30, nothing. I call the dispatcher, and he tells me the driver stopped for breakfast and got stuck in a drive-thru line. A drive-thru! When you have a hundred yards of concrete on the way! So the pump finally shows up at 8:15, but by then, the first three mixer trucks are already lined up on the street, and the drivers are getting antsy because their drums have been spinning for forty-five minutes. And then the concrete foreman—this guy named Sal—he starts arguing with the pump operator about where to set the outriggers, because Sal didn't clear the scrap rebar out of the staging area the day before like he was supposed to. So now they're yelling at each other over the sound of the diesel engines, and I'm just watching my pour window close while paying these guys by the hour to stand around and argue...

Wait. Sorry. I lost track again. My brain just keeps... wandering off. What were you asking? Moving slow or being restless? Yeah, I don't really know. I just feel drained. All the time.

Worse than now? I... I don't really think so. I honestly don't know how it could get much worse. This feels like rock bottom. 

Actually, speaking of rock bottom, our excavation crew on the west side project literally hit bedrock three feet shallower than the geological survey said we would. Three feet! Do you know what that does to a grading plan? The whole site was supposed to balance—cut and fill, right? You take the dirt from the high side, move it to the low side, and you don't have to truck any material in or out. It's basic dirt work. But no, suddenly we're renting a massive hydraulic breaker attachment for the excavator, which costs an absolute fortune by the day. And the noise... my god, the noise. It just pounds and pounds all day long, shaking your teeth loose. The neighbors in the subdivision next door started calling the city to complain, so the city is threatening a stop-work order. And the operator—this kid who looks like he's maybe nineteen—keeps blowing hydraulic lines because he's pushing the machine way too hard. I had to stand out there in the mud yesterday for three hours waiting for the heavy equipment mechanic to bring a replacement hose, just watching money burn while this kid sits in the cab playing on his phone...

Man. I'm sorry. I did it again. My brain just won't stay on track today. What did you ask me? If it's ever been worse? Yeah... no. I don't think so. I've never felt this low before.

I just don't have it in me anymore.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this way, like you've stopped caring about things you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: I'd say... it's been about a year and seven months. It’s honestly worse than dealing with the city permit office. At least with the city, you know which clerk to butter up with a box of donuts, you know? But these insurance reps just read from a script. It reminds me of this safety inspector we had on site last month. Guy walks around with his little clipboard, completely ignoring the actual structural hazards, just looking to check his little boxes. He tried to cite us for an extension cord that wasn't even plugged in. I had to walk him over to the generator and physically show him that the cord was dead, and he still wanted to argue with me about the OSHA manual... \n\nBut, uh... sorry. My brain is just... I don't know. What were we talking about? Oh, right, how long. Yeah, about nineteen months since she got sick. That's when I stopped caring.\n\nDoctor: Do you still find any pleasure in the things you're doing, even if you're just going through the motions?\n\nPatient: No, not really. That's the thing. I'm just... I'm just empty when I do them. Like the chess with the kids. I move the pieces, but I don't care if I win or lose. I used to love teaching them strategy, you know? Setting up traps, showing them how to control the center of the board. It’s a lot like managing a job site, actually. You have to anticipate where the bottlenecks are going to be before they happen. If you don't stage your materials right, your framing crew is just standing around costing you money while they wait for the lumber delivery. And don't even get me started on the concrete guys. They always want to pour on a Friday afternoon, which means if the inspector is running late, you're paying overtime for everyone to just sit there and watch it cure. I had a guy last year try to tell me the mix was too dry, and I had to get the slump cone out myself to prove him wrong...\n\nWait, what did you ask me? Oh, about enjoying things. Right. No, there's no pleasure in it. The chess, the fishing, the woodshop... it all just feels like work now. I'm just doing it because I feel like I'm supposed to, not because I want to.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself moving or speaking more slowly than you used to, or do you feel restless and can't sit still?\n\nPatient: I haven't really noticed anything like that. I mean, maybe I'm a little slower getting out of bed, but I don't think I'm moving or talking in slow motion or anything. And I'm not pacing the halls, if that's what you mean. Though, speaking of pacing, you should see the new apprentice we got on the Route 9 job. Kid cannot stand still for two seconds. He's always bouncing on his heels, twirling his hammer... it drives the older guys crazy. I keep telling him, \"Look, kid, you're burning energy you're gonna need at 3 PM when we're trying to finish framing the second floor.\" It's like these young guys come out of trade school thinking it's all go, go, go, but construction is a marathon, not a sprint. You have to pace yourself, especially in the summer heat. We had a guy pass out last July because he thought he could pound energy drinks instead of water... \n\nBut, uh, anyway. What was the question? Oh, me moving slow or being restless. Yeah, no, I haven't really noticed anything like that with myself.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel guilty or blame yourself for things that have happened?\n\nPatient: Yeah. Yeah, all the time. I just... I keep thinking I've completely failed my wife, you know? And the kids. And even the guys at work. If anything goes wrong—like a missed doctor's appointment for her, or a cost overrun on a job, or even a minor safety issue—I just take it as proof that I'm completely useless. Like everyone would just be better off without me in charge of things. \n\nSpeaking of cost overruns, the price of copper pipe right now is just absolutely insane. We had to re-bid this entire plumbing phase on a commercial build last week because the supplier jacked up the rates by twenty percent overnight. Twenty percent! It completely blew up the budget. And the client, of course, thinks we're trying to fleece them. I had to sit in this three-hour meeting with their architects—who, by the way, designed a drainage system that defies the laws of gravity—trying to explain supply chain economics to them. It's like talking to a brick wall. Actually, a brick wall would be better, because at least a mason knows how to lay it straight. These architects draw lines on a computer and expect the real world to just bend to their software...\n\nUh... wait, what was I saying? Oh, right. Guilt. Yeah, I just... I feel like a failure. Like I'm letting everyone down, and it's all my fault.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever thought that you'd be better off dead or wished you wouldn't wake up in the morning?\n\nPatient: Yeah... yeah, I do. Especially on those early drives. I usually head out to the site around 5 AM, before anyone else is even awake. And I just catch myself thinking it would be so much easier if I just didn't make it there. \n\nThe roads are completely empty then, you know? Just me and the heavy haulers. Actually, speaking of the haulers, the DOT completely botched the routing for that new overpass project on 95. They shut down the main artery, so now you've got these massive flatbeds carrying steel girders trying to navigate two-lane county roads. It’s ridiculous. I was stuck behind one for forty-five minutes on Tuesday. And the worst part is, we had a crane operator sitting idle at the site, and those guys charge out at two hundred bucks an hour, minimum four hours. You can't just tell a crane operator to clock out while you wait for the steel to show up. The logistics on this job are just a total nightmare right now. The general contractor didn't even bother to secure the wide-load permits until the day before, so now everyone is scrambling and pointing fingers...\n\nWait, sorry. I'm doing it again. What did you ask me? Oh. Right. Waking up. \n\nYeah, on those early drives in the dark... sometimes the thought of just steering my truck off the road... it scares me. Because it just feels too close, you know? Like it really wouldn't take much at all.\n\nDoctor: Have you made any plans for how you would end your life if you decided to go through with it?\n\nPatient: No, no... I haven't actually made a plan or anything. It's just... thoughts, you know? I haven't figured out a way or set a date or whatever. \n\nThough, speaking of dates and schedules, I don't even know how I'd find the time to plan anything right now. The scheduling software the front office is making us use is a complete joke. They rolled out this new cloud-based system last month, supposed to streamline everything, right? Supposed to sync the sub-contractors, the material deliveries, the inspections... all in one place. But the interface is so clunky, it takes me twice as long just to log a daily report. And it keeps crashing! Just yesterday, I spent an hour inputting the drywall schedule for the south wing, and the whole thing just froze. Poof. Gone. I had to call IT, and you know how that goes. You get some guy in a call center who asks if you've tried turning it off and on again. I'm standing there in a hard hat, trying to coordinate thirty guys who are waiting on me, and I'm supposed to reboot my tablet while the clock is ticking. It's ridiculous. The old whiteboards we used in the trailer worked perfectly fine for twenty years, but no, we have to \"modernize\" because some VP went to a seminar...\n\nUh, sorry. My mind just keeps wandering today. What were we talking about? Plans? Right. No, I haven't... I haven't made any plans to actually do it. It's just the thoughts.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any significant changes in your appetite or sleep patterns recently?\n\nPatient: Appetite? Yeah, it's... I don't really have one right now. Most days food just tastes like cardboard, honestly. I'll completely skip breakfast, and then even if I've been on my feet at a job site all day, I just sort of pick at my dinner. I've actually had to bring my belt in a couple of notches lately, and I'm not even trying to lose weight. \n\nThough, speaking of eating at the site... the lunch truck we usually get, this guy Sal who runs it? He's been getting absolutely hammered by city code enforcement. They completely changed the commercial parking zones on the west side of the project last month, so now he can't park by the main gate anymore. He has to set up three blocks down the street. And my guys are furious, because they only get thirty minutes for lunch, right? So it takes them ten minutes just to walk down there and back. The union steward actually barged into my trailer yesterday threatening to file a formal grievance over it. Like I have any control over municipal parking ordinances! I'm trying to coordinate a fifty-yard concrete pour, and this guy is yelling at me about meatball subs. I told him to go take it up with the mayor. It's just constant... everyone expects you to solve their problems for them, no matter how ridiculous...\n\nUh... sorry. I completely lost my train of thought again. What did you ask me? Sleep? Or... oh, appetite. Right. Yeah, I just... I don't eat much. Like I said, nothing really tastes good anyway.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any periods in the past two weeks where you felt somewhat better, even if only for a few days?\n\nPatient: Better? No... no, not really. It's just been a constant heavy fog, every single day. I can't remember a day in the last couple of weeks where I woke up and actually felt... okay. \n\nAlthough, speaking of weeks... the timeline on this current build is completely shot. We were supposed to have the framing done by the 15th, but the lumber yard shorted us on the two-by-sixes. And not just by a little bit, they missed an entire pallet. So I've got my framing crew standing around, twiddling their thumbs, while I'm on the phone with the supplier trying to track down this missing pallet. And the guy on the phone—I swear he had no idea what he was doing—he kept telling me it was marked as delivered. I'm looking at the drop zone, and I'm telling him, \"Buddy, there is no wood here.\" Turns out, the driver dropped it at a completely different job site three miles away. Some residential development. So then we had to send one of our guys with a flatbed to go retrieve our own materials from a competitor's site. The site super over there was giving him a hard time about it, too, like we were trying to steal from them. I had to get on the phone with him and explain that his supplier is an idiot... \n\nWait. Sorry. I'm rambling again. What were we talking about? Feeling better? Yeah. No, I haven't had any days where I felt better. It's just... it's all just heavy. All the time.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any thoughts about self-harm or suicide in the past week?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I... I guess so. Mostly in the mornings. On those early drives before anyone else is awake, I'm just sitting in the truck and... \n\nActually, speaking of the drive, the city has completely torn up the interstate near the downtown exit. It is a total nightmare. They've had the right two lanes closed for three weeks for this repaving project, but I swear I haven't seen a single crew out there actually working since last Tuesday. They just leave the orange barrels up and these massive concrete barriers that pinch the lane down to almost nothing. And the guys driving those big dump trucks? They don't care, they just merge right over without looking. I had to slam on my brakes the other day because some guy pulling a trailer full of bobcat attachments just cut across the solid white line. It's like nobody knows how to drive in a construction zone anymore. And the suspension on my rig is already shot from hauling heavy gear around the dirt lots, so hitting those uneven pavement transitions at sixty miles an hour feels like I'm snapping an axle every single morning. I told our fleet manager we need to get the shocks replaced, but he's dragging his feet on the purchase order, trying to save a few bucks...\n\nSorry. Man, I'm doing it again. My head is just... it's all over the place. What did you ask? Thoughts about... right. Self-harm. \n\nSo, yeah, on those early drives... I just catch myself thinking it would be so much easier if I just didn't make it there. Just... didn't arrive. And sometimes the thought of just steering off the road... it scares me, honestly. Because it feels too close. Like I might actually just let the wheel go.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any significant weight loss or gain recently?\n\nPatient: Weight? Yeah, I think I mentioned it earlier, actually. I'm definitely losing weight. I've had to pull my belt in a couple of notches lately, and I'm not even trying to. It's just... food tastes like cardboard most days, so I'll skip breakfast entirely, and then I just pick at whatever I have for dinner, even after being on my feet at the site all day.\n\nSpeaking of the site, we had the city inspector come out yesterday for the rough-in plumbing inspection on the second floor. And this guy—I swear he just likes to hear himself talk—he spent forty-five minutes lecturing my plumbing sub about the exact angle of the PVC venting. Not that it was wrong, mind you, just that he preferred a different style of routing. My plumber, Gary, he's been doing this for thirty years, and he's just standing there, biting his tongue, turning bright red. I honestly thought Gary was going to throw a pipe wrench at the guy. And the worst part is, the inspector wouldn't sign off on the permit until we moved this one vent pipe literally two inches to the left. Two inches! It doesn't change a single thing about how the system functions, but it means Gary has to rip out a whole section of drywall we just put up, cut the pipe, add two new elbows, and glue it all back together. It set us back an entire day, and now the drywallers are mad because their schedule is messed up, and I have to pay them extra to come back and patch the hole. It's just a domino effect of incompetence...\n\nUh, sorry. I'm doing it again, aren't I? Just... drifting off. What was the question? Weight loss. Right. Yeah, my belt is definitely looser. I just don't have the energy to eat, really.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're moving or speaking more slowly than usual, or do you feel restless and can't sit still?\n\nPatient: Moving slow? Um... I don't know, maybe? I guess I haven't really noticed if I'm moving slower, or if I'm restless. I just feel exhausted. \n\nAlthough, speaking of moving slow, you should see the concrete crew we hired for the foundation pour on the new commercial lot. I mean, these guys are moving like molasses in January. We had the pump truck scheduled for 7:00 AM sharp on Tuesday, right? Because you have to beat the heat, otherwise the mix starts setting up too fast in the hose. Well, 7:00 AM rolls around, no pump truck. 7:30, nothing. I call the dispatcher, and he tells me the driver stopped for breakfast and got stuck in a drive-thru line. A drive-thru! When you have a hundred yards of concrete on the way! So the pump finally shows up at 8:15, but by then, the first three mixer trucks are already lined up on the street, and the drivers are getting antsy because their drums have been spinning for forty-five minutes. And then the concrete foreman—this guy named Sal—he starts arguing with the pump operator about where to set the outriggers, because Sal didn't clear the scrap rebar out of the staging area the day before like he was supposed to. So now they're yelling at each other over the sound of the diesel engines, and I'm just watching my pour window close while paying these guys by the hour to stand around and argue...\n\nWait. Sorry. I lost track again. My brain just keeps... wandering off. What were you asking? Moving slow or being restless? Yeah, I don't really know. I just feel drained. All the time.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any periods where your depression was worse than it is now?\n\nPatient: Worse than now? I... I don't really think so. I honestly don't know how it could get much worse. This feels like rock bottom. \n\nActually, speaking of rock bottom, our excavation crew on the west side project literally hit bedrock three feet shallower than the geological survey said we would. Three feet! Do you know what that does to a grading plan? The whole site was supposed to balance—cut and fill, right? You take the dirt from the high side, move it to the low side, and you don't have to truck any material in or out. It's basic dirt work. But no, suddenly we're renting a massive hydraulic breaker attachment for the excavator, which costs an absolute fortune by the day. And the noise... my god, the noise. It just pounds and pounds all day long, shaking your teeth loose. The neighbors in the subdivision next door started calling the city to complain, so the city is threatening a stop-work order. And the operator—this kid who looks like he's maybe nineteen—keeps blowing hydraulic lines because he's pushing the machine way too hard. I had to stand out there in the mud yesterday for three hours waiting for the heavy equipment mechanic to bring a replacement hose, just watching money burn while this kid sits in the cab playing on his phone...\n\nMan. I'm sorry. I did it again. My brain just won't stay on track today. What did you ask me? If it's ever been worse? Yeah... no. I don't think so. I've never felt this low before.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. Getting worse? Over the past few months? Um... yeah, I guess so. It kind of just crept up on me, you know? Like, it started bad, and then it just... kept sliding downhill. 

Speaking of sliding downhill, you should see the erosion control mess we've got at the Oak Creek development. It's an absolute nightmare. The civil engineer swore up and down that the retention pond was sized correctly for a hundred-year rain event. But then we got that storm last week—maybe three inches over two days, nothing crazy—and the whole thing just overflowed. Silt fences? Useless. The water just blew right past them and washed half the topsoil from phase two straight down into the county drainage ditch. Do you know the kind of fines the EPA slaps you with for that? It's astronomical. And my superintendent, Mike, he was supposed to be monitoring the stormwater plan, but he took off early on Friday to go fishing, so nobody was there to throw down extra sandbags when it started pouring. Now I've got the county environmental inspector breathing down my neck, demanding a revised SWPPP by Monday, and I have to hire a specialty vac-truck crew to come suck all the mud out of the county ditch before it dries into concrete...

God, I'm sorry. I'm doing it again. I just can't seem to hold a thought today. It's like my brain is just a sieve. What did you ask? Has it been getting worse? Yeah. Yeah, it definitely has. Just a slow, steady decline into... whatever this is.

I'm just... I'm just empty when I do them. Like the chess with the kids. I move the pieces, but I don't care if I win or lose. I used to love teaching them strategy, you know? Setting up traps, showing them how to control the center of the board. It’s a lot like managing a job site, actually. You have to anticipate where the bottlenecks are going to be before they happen. If you don't stage your materials right, your framing crew is just standing around costing you money while they wait for the lumber delivery. And don't even get me started on the concrete guys. They always want to pour on a Friday afternoon, which means if the inspector is running late, you're paying overtime for everyone to just sit there and watch it cure. I had a guy last year try to tell me the mix was too dry, and I had to get the slump cone out myself to prove him wrong...\n\nWait, what did you ask me? Oh, about enjoying things. Right.
